# Harnessing Agentic Evolution

<div id="abstract1" class="ltx_abstract">

###### Abstract

Agentic evolution has emerged as a powerful paradigm for improving
programs, workflows, and scientific solutions by iteratively generating
candidates, evaluating them, and using feedback to guide future search.
However, existing methods are typically instantiated either as fixed
hand-designed procedures that are modular but rigid, or as
general-purpose agents that flexibly integrate feedback but can drift in
long-horizon evolution. Both forms accumulate rich evidence over time,
including candidates, feedback, traces, and failures, yet lack a stable
interface for organizing this evidence and revising the mechanism that
drives future evolution. We address this limitation by formulating
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">agentic evolution as an interactive
environment</span>, where the accumulated evolution context serves as a
process-level state. We introduce
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">AEvo</span>, a harnessed
meta-editing framework in which a meta-agent observes this state and
acts not by directly proposing the next candidate, but by editing the
procedure or agent context that controls future evolution. This unified
interface enables <span class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">AEvo</span>
to steer both procedure-based and agent-based evolution, making
accumulated evidence actionable for long-horizon search. Empirical
evaluations on agentic and reasoning benchmarks show that
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">AEvo</span> outperforms five
evolution baselines, achieving a
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">26%</span> relative improvement
over the strongest baseline. Across three open-ended optimization tasks,
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">AEvo</span> further
outperforms four evolution baselines and achieves state-of-the-art
performance under the same iteration budget.

</div>

<div id="S1" class="section ltx_section">

## <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_section">1 </span>Introduction

<div id="S1.p1" class="ltx_para">

Agentic evolution reframes LLM-based problem solving as a process of
constructing and revising
solutions \[<a href="#bib.bib17" class="ltx_ref"
title="Advances and challenges in foundation agents: from brain-inspired intelligence to evolutionary, collaborative, and safe systems">16</a>,
<a href="#bib.bib16" class="ltx_ref"
title="A survey of self-evolving agents: on path to artificial super intelligence">7</a>\].
Instead of treating the model only as a generator of candidate answers,
these methods use LLMs, agentic workflows, or coding agents to drive
iterative improvement: produce candidate artifacts, interpret feedback
from evaluation, and influence what the system explores
next \[<a href="#bib.bib25" class="ltx_ref"
title="CORAL: towards autonomous multi-agent evolution for open-ended discovery">22</a>\].
This paradigm has been applied to program
synthesis \[<a href="#bib.bib10" class="ltx_ref"
title="Swe-bench: can language models resolve real-world github issues?">10</a>\],
scientific discovery \[<a href="#bib.bib34" class="ltx_ref"
title="The ai scientist: towards fully automated open-ended scientific discovery">17</a>,
<a href="#bib.bib30" class="ltx_ref"
title="Learning to discover at test time">38</a>\], systems
optimization \[<a href="#bib.bib11" class="ltx_ref"
title="Terminal-bench: a benchmark for ai agents in terminal environments">25</a>,
<a href="#bib.bib6" class="ltx_ref"
title="Interactcomp: evaluating search agents with ambiguous queries">6</a>\],
and agent
self-improvement \[<a href="#bib.bib21" class="ltx_ref" title="Hyperagents">41</a>,
<a href="#bib.bib19" class="ltx_ref"
title="Huxley-g\&quot; odel machine: human-level coding agent development by an approximation of the optimal self-improving machine">27</a>,
<a href="#bib.bib35" class="ltx_ref"
title="AOrchestra: automating sub-agent creation for agentic orchestration">23</a>\].
In this paper, we use <span id="S1.p1.1.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">agentic evolution</span> to broadly refer
to evolution processes whose search behavior is driven by either
structured agentic procedures or general-purpose agents.

</div>

<div id="S1.p2" class="ltx_para">

Existing agentic evolution methods typically instantiate this paradigm
in two ways. In <span id="S1.p2.1.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">procedure-based evolution</span>, a
predefined outer loop controls parent selection, candidate generation,
evaluation, and population update \[<a href="#bib.bib26" class="ltx_ref"
title="Alphaevolve: a coding agent for scientific and algorithmic discovery">18</a>,
<a href="#bib.bib24" class="ltx_ref"
title="Evaluation-driven scaling for scientific discovery">36</a>,
<a href="#bib.bib8" class="ltx_ref"
title="Aflow: automating agentic workflow generation">42</a>\]. This
makes evolution modular and reproducible, but also ties long-horizon
search to fixed selection rules, feedback summaries, and update
heuristics. In <span id="S1.p2.1.2"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">agent-based evolution</span>, a
general-purpose agent manages the search process by observing feedback,
inspecting traces, editing candidates, writing tools, and deciding what
to try next \[<a href="#bib.bib31" class="ltx_ref"
title="Autoresearch: ai agents running research on single-gpu nanochat training automatically">11</a>,
<a href="#bib.bib25" class="ltx_ref"
title="CORAL: towards autonomous multi-agent evolution for open-ended discovery">22</a>\].
This gives evolution greater flexibility, but the agent can drift as
candidates, logs, hypotheses, and intermediate files accumulate. In both
cases, long-horizon evolution remains prone to local optima: procedures
may repeatedly exploit the same hand-designed search pattern, while
agents may overcommit to misleading evidence or stale assumptions in a
growing context.

</div>

<div id="S1.p3" class="ltx_para">

Recent work has tried to address these limitations by either broadening
exploration with collaborative
agents \[<a href="#bib.bib25" class="ltx_ref"
title="CORAL: towards autonomous multi-agent evolution for open-ended discovery">22</a>\]
or making the evolution mechanism
self-modifying \[<a href="#bib.bib21" class="ltx_ref" title="Hyperagents">41</a>\].
These directions show that stronger search context and editable
improvement mechanisms are useful, but they do not by themselves provide
a stable interface for long-horizon evolution. The core challenge is
that evolution accumulates candidates, feedback, traces, failures, and
intermediate decisions over time, yet lacks a unified way to organize
this evidence and revise the mechanism that drives future evolution.

</div>

<figure id="S1.F1" class="ltx_figure">
<img src="/html/2605.13821/assets/x1.png" id="S1.F1.g1"
class="ltx_graphics ltx_centering ltx_img_landscape" width="830"
height="257" alt="Refer to caption" />
<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_figure"><span id="S1.F1.4.1.1"
class="ltx_text" style="font-size:90%;">Figure 1</span>: </span><span
id="S1.F1.5.2" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:90%;"> <span
id="S1.F1.5.2.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Harnessing agentic
evolution as an interactive environment.</span> (a) Procedure-based
evolution runs a fixed loop for selection, optimization, evaluation, and
update. (b) Agent-based evolution lets a general-purpose agent manage
search through feedback, tools, skills, and code actions. (c) <span
id="S1.F1.5.2.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">AEvo</span> treats
the evolution process as an interactive environment. The accumulated
evolution context becomes process-level state, while a meta-agent edits
the underlying procedure or agent operating context that controls future
evolution. </span></figcaption>
</figure>

<div id="S1.p4" class="ltx_para">

We address this challenge by formulating <span id="S1.p4.1.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">agentic evolution as an interactive
environment</span>. As illustrated in
Figure <a href="#S1.F1" class="ltx_ref"
title="Figure 1 ‣ 1 Introduction ‣ Harnessing Agentic Evolution"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">1</span></a>, this view shifts evolution
from an unstructured iterative process into an environment that exposes
process-level state and supports external intervention. The state is the
accumulated evolution context, including candidates, feedback, traces,
failures, costs, and search history. The transition mechanism is the
current evolution mechanism: either an explicit search procedure or the
operating context that shapes a general-purpose agent’s future
decisions. A meta-agent acts on this environment not by generating the
next candidate, but by editing the mechanism that controls how future
evolution proceeds. This makes the same environment view applicable to
both hand-designed procedures and general-purpose evolution agents.

</div>

<div id="S1.p5" class="ltx_para">

Realizing this view requires a harnessed design. The evolution
environment is large, noisy, and constantly changing. Without a stable
interface, a meta-agent may lose track of reliable evidence, revisit old
attempts or make edits whose effects are hard to verify. At the same
time, evaluation and candidate records must remain protected from the
agents that modify the evolution process. These challenges motivate a
harness that makes evolution observable, editable, and externally
governed.

</div>

<div id="S1.p6" class="ltx_para">

We therefore introduce <span id="S1.p6.1.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">AEvo</span>, a harnessed framework
for meta-editing agentic evolution. <span id="S1.p6.1.2"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">AEvo</span> standardizes the
evolution workspace, protects the evaluator, records every evaluated
candidate into a searchable history, and exposes process-level
information to the meta-agent. It then runs evolution through a
two-phase loop. In the meta-editing phase, the meta-agent edits the
current mechanism and specifies how the next segment should run. In the
evolution segment, the updated mechanism runs under this plan and
produces multiple candidates before the next meta-agent intervention.
The same loop can revise both procedure- and agent-based evolution,
reducing the risk of local optima.

</div>

<div id="S1.p7" class="ltx_para">

Our contributions are threefold. (1) <span id="S1.p7.1.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Environment Formulation:</span> We
formulate agentic evolution as an interactive environment, where
accumulated evolution context becomes process-level state and
meta-actions edit the mechanism that drives future evolution. (2)
<span id="S1.p7.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Harnessed
Meta-Editing:</span> We introduce <span id="S1.p7.1.3"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">AEvo</span>, a harnessed framework
for meta-editing agentic evolution that protects evaluation, records
evaluated candidates, and supports coarse-grained intervention through
meta-editing phases and evolution segments. (3) <span id="S1.p7.1.4"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Cross-Form Instantiation and
Evaluation:</span> We instantiate the same framework on both
procedure-based and agent-based evolution, showing that
<span id="S1.p7.1.5" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">AEvo</span> can
revise either explicit procedure components or agent operating contexts.
On standard agentic and reasoning benchmarks, <span id="S1.p7.1.6"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">AEvo</span> outperforms five
evolution baselines and achieves a 26% relative improvement over the
strongest baseline. On three open-ended optimization tasks,
<span id="S1.p7.1.7" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">AEvo</span>
outperforms four evolution baselines and achieves state-of-the-art
performance under the same iteration budget.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S2" class="section ltx_section">

## <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_section">2 </span>Related Work

<div id="S2.p1" class="ltx_para">

<span id="S2.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Agentic
Evolution.</span> A growing line of work uses LLMs and agents to
iteratively improve artifacts through generation, feedback, and
revision \[<a href="#bib.bib3" class="ltx_ref"
title="Alpha-sql: zero-shot text-to-sql using monte carlo tree search">15</a>,
<a href="#bib.bib5" class="ltx_ref"
title="DeepEye: a steerable self-driving data agent system">14</a>,
<a href="#bib.bib4" class="ltx_ref"
title="DeepEye-sql: A software-engineering-inspired text-to-sql framework">13</a>,
<a href="#bib.bib2" class="ltx_ref"
title="AutoWebWorld: synthesizing infinite verifiable web environments via finite state machines">28</a>\].
Prompt methods optimize language-model programs or feedback-driven
prompts, including DSPy \[<a href="#bib.bib29" class="ltx_ref"
title="DSPy: compiling declarative language model calls into self-improving pipelines">12</a>\],
SPO \[<a href="#bib.bib7" class="ltx_ref"
title="Self-supervised prompt optimization">30</a>\],
TextGrad \[<a href="#bib.bib15" class="ltx_ref"
title="Textgrad: automatic&quot; differentiation&quot; via text">37</a>\],
and GEPA \[<a href="#bib.bib9" class="ltx_ref"
title="Gepa: reflective prompt evolution can outperform reinforcement learning">1</a>\].
Another line studies the automated design and evolution of agentic
systems and workflows, such as
ADAS \[<a href="#bib.bib13" class="ltx_ref"
title="Automated design of agentic systems">9</a>\], Darwin Gödel
Machine \[<a href="#bib.bib12" class="ltx_ref"
title="Darwin godel machine: open-ended evolution of self-improving agents">40</a>\],
Huxley-Gödel Machine \[<a href="#bib.bib19" class="ltx_ref"
title="Huxley-g\&quot; odel machine: human-level coding agent development by an approximation of the optimal self-improving machine">27</a>\],
AFlow \[<a href="#bib.bib8" class="ltx_ref"
title="Aflow: automating agentic workflow generation">42</a>\],
RobustFlow \[<a href="#bib.bib36" class="ltx_ref"
title="Robustflow: towards robust agentic workflow generation">32</a>\],
and SkillRL \[<a href="#bib.bib38" class="ltx_ref"
title="Skillrl: evolving agents via recursive skill-augmented reinforcement learning">29</a>\].
Recent open-ended discovery systems further apply evolutionary search to
scientific and algorithmic discovery, including
AlphaEvolve \[<a href="#bib.bib26" class="ltx_ref"
title="Alphaevolve: a coding agent for scientific and algorithmic discovery">18</a>\],
OpenEvolve \[<a href="#bib.bib27" class="ltx_ref"
title="OpenEvolve: an open-source evolutionary coding agent">24</a>\],
TTS-Discover \[<a href="#bib.bib30" class="ltx_ref"
title="Learning to discover at test time">38</a>\],
CORAL \[<a href="#bib.bib25" class="ltx_ref"
title="CORAL: towards autonomous multi-agent evolution for open-ended discovery">22</a>\],
SimpleTES \[<a href="#bib.bib24" class="ltx_ref"
title="Evaluation-driven scaling for scientific discovery">36</a>\], and
ASI-Evolve \[<a href="#bib.bib39" class="ltx_ref"
title="ASI-evolve: ai accelerates ai">33</a>\]. However, their search
behavior is typically controlled either by fixed procedures or by agents
directly managing candidate generation. In contrast,
<span id="S2.p1.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">AEvo</span>
treats the evolution process itself as an interactive environment and
studies how to steer the mechanism that controls future search.

</div>

<div id="S2.p2" class="ltx_para">

<span id="S2.p2.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Agentic
Meta-Evolution.</span> Early meta-learning work showed that the learning
rule itself can be optimized, for example by learning recurrent
reinforcement-learning dynamics \[<a href="#bib.bib32" class="ltx_ref"
title="Learning to reinforcement learn">26</a>\] or evolving
policy-gradient objectives \[<a href="#bib.bib33" class="ltx_ref"
title="Evolved policy gradients">8</a>\]. Recent agentic systems extend
this idea to editable agent programs and memory systems. HyperAgents
study self-referential agent programs in which both task-solving
behavior and the meta-improvement mechanism can be
modified \[<a href="#bib.bib21" class="ltx_ref" title="Hyperagents">41</a>\].
MemEvolve and ALMA similarly explore meta-evolution over agent memory
designs \[<a href="#bib.bib18" class="ltx_ref"
title="MemEvolve: meta-evolution of agent memory systems">39</a>,
<a href="#bib.bib28" class="ltx_ref"
title="Learning to continually learn via meta-learning agentic memory designs">31</a>\].
Unlike HyperAgents, which internalize meta-improvement within a
self-modifying agent program, <span id="S2.p2.1.2"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">AEvo</span> treats agentic evolution
as an interactive environment observed and edited through an external
harness, covering both hand-designed procedures and general-purpose
agents while keeping evaluation and candidate recording externally
governed.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S3" class="section ltx_section">

## <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_section">3 </span>Problem Formulation

<div id="S3.SS1" class="section ltx_subsection">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">3.1 </span>Agentic Evolution

<div id="S3.SS1.p1" class="ltx_para">

We formulate agentic evolution as a process for optimizing an artifact
through repeated improvement rounds. Let $`x\in\mathcal{X}`$ denote the
object being optimized, such as a program, prompt, workflow, skill,
tool, or agent component. We use $`r`$ to index the evolution round.
Each round produces a round context $`c_{r}`$, which contains the
candidates generated in that round, their evaluation results, execution
traces, failures, costs, and any intermediate information produced
during optimization. The accumulated evolution context after $`r`$
rounds is denoted as

|     |                                             |     |
|-----|---------------------------------------------|-----|
|     |                                             
       ``` math                                     
       \mathcal{C}_{r}=(c_{1},c_{2},\ldots,c_{r}).  
       ```                                          |     |

Finally, let $`\Pi`$ denote the optimization mechanism that advances
evolution:

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  | 
``` math
c_{r}=\Pi(\mathcal{C}_{r-1}),\qquad\mathcal{C}_{r}=\mathcal{C}_{r-1}\oplus c_{r},
``` |  |

where $`\oplus`$ appends the newly produced round context to the
accumulated evolution context. $`\Pi`$ does not have to be a fixed
algorithm; it can also be an agentic process that reads the history,
reasons over feedback, and decides how to generate the next candidate.
Thus, $`\Pi`$ represents the mechanism by which search is continued from
the current evolution context.

</div>

<div id="S3.SS1.p2" class="ltx_para">

Under this formulation, existing agentic evolution methods mainly differ
in how $`\Pi`$ is instantiated. In <span id="S3.SS1.p2.5.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">procedure-based evolution </span>,
$`\Pi`$ is a predefined outer loop whose behavior is mainly determined
by selection and optimization: the selection rule chooses previous
candidates or contexts from $`\mathcal{C}_{r-1}`$, while the
optimization operator generates new candidates from the selected
information. Evaluation assigns scores, traces, and feedback to the
generated candidates, providing signals for future selection and update.
In <span id="S3.SS1.p2.5.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">agent-based
evolution</span>, $`\Pi`$ is instead implemented by a general-purpose
agent. Rather than following fixed selection-and-optimization rules, the
agent reads the accumulated context $`\mathcal{C}_{r-1}`$ and decides
what to do next, such as inspecting feedback, comparing candidates,
modifying artifacts, writing tools, or generating new attempts. Thus,
procedure-based evolution specifies search control explicitly but
rigidly, while agent-based evolution leaves search control implicit in
the agent’s context-conditioned behavior.

</div>

<div id="S3.SS1.p3" class="ltx_para">

In both cases, evolution proceeds by repeatedly applying $`\Pi`$ while
accumulating context $`\mathcal{C}_{r}`$. This context records not only
the candidates produced by evolution, but also how search has unfolded
through evaluation results, traces, failures, costs, and intermediate
artifacts. The next subsection uses this accumulated context to define
an environment view of evolution.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S3.SS2" class="section ltx_subsection">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">3.2 </span>Evolution as an Interactive Environment

<div id="S3.SS2.p1" class="ltx_para">

We treat the evolution process itself as an interactive environment for
a meta-agent. At round $`r`$, the state of this environment is defined
by the round index and the accumulated evolution context:

|     |                            |     |
|-----|----------------------------|-----|
|     |                            
       ``` math                    
       s_{r}=(r,\mathcal{C}_{r}).  
       ```                         |     |

When the optimization mechanism may change across rounds, we write the
current mechanism as $`\Pi_{r}`$. This mechanism specifies the
transition of the environment. Without intervention, the next round is
produced by applying the current mechanism to the current context:

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  | 
``` math
c_{r+1}=\Pi_{r}(\mathcal{C}_{r}),\qquad s_{r+1}=(r+1,\mathcal{C}_{r}\oplus c_{r+1}).
``` |  |

Thus, $`\Pi_{r}`$ is the transition rule that determines how the
evolution process continues.

</div>

<div id="S3.SS2.p2" class="ltx_para">

To interact with this environment, we introduce a meta-agent $`M`$. The
role of $`\Pi_{r}`$ is to continue the candidate search, while the role
of $`M`$ is to act on the evolution process that governs this search.
Since the full state $`s_{r}`$ can be large and noisy, the meta-agent
receives an observation extracted from the state:

|     |                                            |     |
|-----|--------------------------------------------|-----|
|     |                                            
       ``` math                                    
       o_{r}=\Phi(s_{r})=\Phi(r,\mathcal{C}_{r}),  
       ```                                         |     |

where $`\Phi`$ summarizes relevant information from the accumulated
context, such as progress, repeated failures, invalid attempts, cost
patterns, or redundant search directions.

</div>

<div id="S3.SS2.p3" class="ltx_para">

Given this observation, the meta-agent produces an edit action:

|     |                 |     |
|-----|-----------------|-----|
|     |                 
       ``` math         
       a_{r}=M(o_{r}).  
       ```              |     |

The action does not directly become the next candidate. Instead, it
modifies the transition rule of the evolution environment:

|     |                                         |     |
|-----|-----------------------------------------|-----|
|     |                                         
       ``` math                                 
       \Pi_{r+1}=\mathrm{Edit}(\Pi_{r},a_{r}).  
       ```                                      |     |

The edited mechanism is then used to continue evolution:

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  | 
``` math
c_{r+1}=\Pi_{r+1}(\mathcal{C}_{r}),\qquad\mathcal{C}_{r+1}=\mathcal{C}_{r}\oplus c_{r+1}.
``` |  |

In this sense, we formulate agentic evolution as an environment in which
the state is the accumulated evolution context, the observation is a
summary of this context, and the action edits the mechanism that
controls future search.

</div>

<div id="S3.SS2.p4" class="ltx_para">

This formulation applies to both forms of agentic evolution. For
procedure-based evolution , editing $`\Pi_{r}`$ changes explicit
components such as selection, optimization, feedback use, budget
allocation, or update rules. For agent-based evolution, editing
$`\Pi_{r}`$ changes the agentic context that shapes future decisions,
such as skills, goals, tools, feedback format, or execution context. In
both cases, the meta-agent steers evolution not by proposing one more
candidate, but by modifying how subsequent search is carried out.
Section <a href="#S4" class="ltx_ref"
title="4 Methodology ‣ Harnessing Agentic Evolution"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">4</span></a> describes the system design
used to instantiate this formulation.

</div>

</div>

</div>

<div id="S4" class="section ltx_section">

## <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_section">4 </span>Methodology

<figure id="S4.F2" class="ltx_figure">
<img src="/html/2605.13821/assets/x2.png" id="S4.F2.g1"
class="ltx_graphics ltx_centering ltx_img_landscape" width="830"
height="317" alt="Refer to caption" />
<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_figure"><span id="S4.F2.9.4.1"
class="ltx_text" style="font-size:90%;">Figure 2</span>: </span><span
id="S4.F2.6.3" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:90%;"> <span
id="S4.F2.6.3.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Architecture of <span
id="S4.F2.6.3.1.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">AEvo</span>.</span> The harness runs
evolution segments under the current mechanism <span
class="math inline"><em>Π</em><sub><em>r</em></sub></span>, protects
evaluation, and records structured evidence. A meta-agent observes this
evidence to edit <span
class="math inline"><em>Π</em><sub><em>r</em></sub></span> into <span
class="math inline"><em>Π</em><sub><em>r</em> + 1</sub></span> and set
the next run plan, enabling coarse-grained intervention over both
procedures and agent contexts. </span></figcaption>
</figure>

<div id="S4.p1" class="ltx_para">

Figure <a href="#S4.F2" class="ltx_ref"
title="Figure 2 ‣ 4 Methodology ‣ Harnessing Agentic Evolution"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">2</span></a> illustrates
<span id="S4.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">AEvo</span>.
<span id="S4.p1.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">AEvo</span>
instantiates the environment view in
Section <a href="#S3" class="ltx_ref"
title="3 Problem Formulation ‣ Harnessing Agentic Evolution"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">3</span></a> as a harnessed loop that
alternates between a *meta-editing phase* and an *evolution segment*. In
the meta-editing phase, the meta-agent updates the current evolution
mechanism $`\Pi_{r}`$ and specifies how the next segment should run,
including its iteration budget and stopping conditions. In the evolution
segment, the updated mechanism runs under this plan and may produce
multiple evaluated candidates before the next meta-agent intervention.
Thus, one meta-edit can govern a segment of future evolution rather than
a single candidate.

</div>

<div id="S4.SS1" class="section ltx_subsection">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">4.1 </span>Design of <span id="S4.SS1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">AEvo</span>

<div id="S4.SS1.p1" class="ltx_para">

<span id="S4.SS1.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Meta-editing
phase.</span> The meta-editing phase decides both
<span id="S4.SS1.p1.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">what to
change</span> and <span id="S4.SS1.p1.1.3"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">how to continue</span>. The meta-agent
can be any coding-capable agent that can inspect a workspace, edit
files, execute commands, and follow the <span id="S4.SS1.p1.1.4"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">AEvo</span> meta-agent skill
specification, such as Claude
Code \[<a href="#bib.bib45" class="ltx_ref" title="Claude Code">3</a>\],
Codex \[<a href="#bib.bib46" class="ltx_ref" title="Codex">19</a>\], or
open-source coding agents \[<a href="#bib.bib43" class="ltx_ref"
title="SWE-agent: agent-computer interfaces enable automated software engineering">34</a>,
<a href="#bib.bib44" class="ltx_ref"
title="OpenCode: the open source AI coding agent">20</a>\]. Given the
current workspace, it inspects the accumulated history, then produces a
meta-action consisting of a workspace edit and a run plan. The workspace
edit modifies files that define $`\Pi_{r}`$, such as procedure code,
prompts, skills, goals, tools, feedback formats, validators, notes, or
execution context. The run plan specifies how the next evolution segment
should proceed, including the allowed iterations, budget use, and
stopping conditions.

</div>

<div id="S4.SS1.p2" class="ltx_para">

This design makes the meta-agent a process-level editor rather than a
candidate generator: it changes the mechanism and conditions under which
future candidates are produced. When evolution is productive, the
meta-agent may allocate more iterations to the current mechanism; when
it repeatedly produces invalid candidates, redundant attempts, or
irrelevant exploration, the meta-agent may stop the segment and revise
$`\Pi_{r}`$ before continuing. The full meta-agent skill specification
and pseudocode of the meta-editing loop are provided in
Appendix <a href="#A3.SS2.SSS3" class="ltx_ref"
title="C.2.3 Meta-Agent Skill Excerpt ‣ C.2 Initialization Details ‣ Appendix C Additional Experimental Details ‣ Harnessing Agentic Evolution"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">C.2.3</span></a>.

</div>

<div id="S4.SS1.p3" class="ltx_para">

<span id="S4.SS1.p3.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Harnessed
evolution segment.</span> An evolution segment is the interval executed
after a meta-edit. It runs the current mechanism $`\Pi_{r}`$ under the
run plan produced by the meta-agent. Depending on the setting, this
segment may consist of several rounds of a procedure, or an inner-agent
session that produces multiple candidate attempts. Each candidate
submitted for official evaluation passes through the harness-controlled
evaluator, and the resulting artifact, score, trace, failure
information, cost, and provenance are appended to the candidate history.

</div>

<div id="S4.SS1.p4" class="ltx_para">

The harness provides the stable boundary needed for reliable
meta-editing. It organizes candidates, logs, traces, evaluation records,
meta-agent instructions, and editable evolution components into a fixed
workspace layout. To prevent reward hacking, the evaluator is isolated
from both the evolution agent and the meta-agent: agents can submit
candidates, but they cannot inspect evaluator internals, access hidden
benchmark artifacts, or directly write official scores. The harness
further exposes a command-line interface for initializing workspaces,
launching evolution segments, inspecting recent status and candidate
history, and continuing the current process. Thus, the harness does not
decide how evolution should improve; it provides the protected and
inspectable interface through which evolution can be observed, edited by
the meta-agent, recorded, and resumed.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S4.SS2" class="section ltx_subsection">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">4.2 </span>Instantiating <span id="S4.SS2.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">AEvo</span>

<div id="S4.SS2.p1" class="ltx_para">

<span id="S4.SS2.p1.2.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">AEvo</span>
applies the same two-phase loop to both forms of agentic evolution. The
outer loop is unchanged: the meta-agent edits the current mechanism
$`\Pi_{r}`$ and specifies how the next evolution segment should run. The
difference lies in what $`\Pi_{r}`$ consists of.

</div>

<div id="S4.SS2.p2" class="ltx_para">

<span id="S4.SS2.p2.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Procedure-based
evolution.</span> For procedure-based evolution, $`\Pi_{r}`$ is an
explicit evolution procedure. It defines how previous candidates or
contexts are selected, how new candidates are generated, how evaluation
feedback is used, and how candidate history is updated. A meta-action
therefore edits the procedure itself, such as revising the selection
strategy, changing the optimization operator, altering the feedback
summary, adding local filtering or retry logic, adjusting budget use, or
repairing candidate management. The edited procedure then controls the
next evolution segment, which may run for multiple rounds before the
next meta-agent intervention.

</div>

<div id="S4.SS2.p3" class="ltx_para">

<span id="S4.SS2.p3.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Agent-based
evolution.</span> For agent-based evolution, $`\Pi_{r}`$ is the
operating context of a general-purpose evolution agent, including goals,
skills, tools, memory files, shared notes, validators, and execution
setup. A meta-action therefore edits the conditions under which the next
inner-agent session will evolve, such as revising a skill, rewriting the
session goal, changing how evaluator feedback is presented, or
reorganizing shared notes. The inner agent remains responsible for
generating candidates, while <span id="S4.SS2.p3.1.2"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">AEvo</span> revises the context that
shapes future evolution.

</div>

</div>

</div>

<div id="S5" class="section ltx_section">

## <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_section">5 </span>Experiments

<div id="S5.SS1" class="section ltx_subsection">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">5.1 </span>Settings

<div id="S5.SS1.p1" class="ltx_para">

<span id="S5.SS1.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Tasks.</span> We
evaluate <span id="S5.SS1.p1.1.2"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">AEvo</span> on two standard
benchmarks, Terminal-Bench \[<a href="#bib.bib11" class="ltx_ref"
title="Terminal-bench: a benchmark for ai agents in terminal environments">25</a>\]
and ARC-AGI-2 \[<a href="#bib.bib14" class="ltx_ref"
title="Arc-agi-2: a new challenge for frontier ai reasoning systems">5</a>\],
and three open-ended optimization tasks, circle_packing_26
(CP26)\[<a href="#bib.bib41" class="ltx_ref"
title="Packing circles in a square: a review and new results">21</a>\],
autocorrelation_second (AC2)\[<a href="#bib.bib42" class="ltx_ref"
title="An improved example for an autoconvolution inequality">4</a>\],
and Anthropic’s Kernel optimization
task \[<a href="#bib.bib40" class="ltx_ref"
title="Anthropic’s Original Performance Take-Home">2</a>\]. Together,
these tasks cover agentic problem solving, abstract reasoning, and
code-evolving open-ended optimization. Detailed task definitions and
evaluation protocols are given in
Appendix <a href="#A3.SS1" class="ltx_ref"
title="C.1 Task and Metric Details ‣ Appendix C Additional Experimental Details ‣ Harnessing Agentic Evolution"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">C.1</span></a>.

</div>

<div id="S5.SS1.p2" class="ltx_para">

<span id="S5.SS1.p2.1.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Baselines.</span> We group baselines into
three families: single-agent inference, agent-based evolution, and
procedure-based evolution. On Terminal-Bench and ARC-AGI-2, we compare
against one-shot ReAct \[<a href="#bib.bib37" class="ltx_ref"
title="React: synergizing reasoning and acting in language models">35</a>\]
and five procedure-based evolution baselines:
ADAS \[<a href="#bib.bib13" class="ltx_ref"
title="Automated design of agentic systems">9</a>\],
DGM \[<a href="#bib.bib12" class="ltx_ref"
title="Darwin godel machine: open-ended evolution of self-improving agents">40</a>\],
AFlow \[<a href="#bib.bib8" class="ltx_ref"
title="Aflow: automating agentic workflow generation">42</a>\],
SPO \[<a href="#bib.bib7" class="ltx_ref"
title="Self-supervised prompt optimization">30</a>\], and
GEPA \[<a href="#bib.bib9" class="ltx_ref"
title="Gepa: reflective prompt evolution can outperform reinforcement learning">1</a>\].
On the three open-ended tasks, we compare against two agent-based
evolution baselines, Codex and Claude Code, and two procedure-based
evolution baselines, OpenEvolve \[<a href="#bib.bib27" class="ltx_ref"
title="OpenEvolve: an open-source evolutionary coding agent">24</a>\]
and
HyperAgents \[<a href="#bib.bib21" class="ltx_ref" title="Hyperagents">41</a>\].
This setup lets us compare both variants of <span id="S5.SS1.p2.1.2"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">AEvo</span> against systems that
either keep a fixed search procedure or rely on an agent to improve
artifacts directly.

</div>

<div id="S5.SS1.p3" class="ltx_para">

<span id="S5.SS1.p3.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Implementation
Details.</span> <span id="S5.SS1.p3.1.2"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">AEvo</span> is instantiated in two
forms. In the procedure-based setting, a meta-agent edits the evolution
procedure while leaving the task evaluator fixed. In the agent-based
setting, the meta-agent steers a coding-agent harness through prompts,
notes, and reusable utilities. We use Claude Code and Codex as the
meta-agent interfaces, backed by Claude-Opus-4.7 and GPT-5.4 as the
optimization models. For Terminal-Bench and ARC-AGI-2, candidate
execution uses Gemini-3-Flash. All models are accessed through APIs.
Full hyperparameter settings, round budgets, early-stopping criteria,
and initialization details are given in
Appendix <a href="#A2" class="ltx_ref"
title="Appendix B Implementation Details ‣ Harnessing Agentic Evolution"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">B</span></a>.

</div>

<div id="S5.SS1.p4" class="ltx_para">

<span id="S5.SS1.p4.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Metrics.</span>
For Terminal-Bench and ARC-AGI-2, we report task score and the first
optimization round that reaches the best score. Results are summarized
with Avg@3 over three independent runs. For the open-ended tasks, we
report the task-native objective with Best@3 over three runs, together
with the first round that reaches the best result (Best R.) and the
average dollar cost per optimization round (\$/R). Exact task-specific
objectives and cost computation are provided in
Appendix <a href="#A3.SS1" class="ltx_ref"
title="C.1 Task and Metric Details ‣ Appendix C Additional Experimental Details ‣ Harnessing Agentic Evolution"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">C.1</span></a>.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S5.SS2" class="section ltx_subsection">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">5.2 </span>Main Results

class="ltx_text ltx_framed ltx_framed_underline">underline</span> mark

class="ltx_text" style="font-size:90%;"> </span><span
class="math inline"><sub><code>Procedure</code></sub></span></td>
<td id="S5.T2.4.4.3"
class="ltx_td ltx_align_left ltx_border_bb ltx_border_t"

<td id="S5.T2.4.4.4"
class="ltx_td ltx_align_center ltx_border_bb ltx_border_t"
style="padding: 0.7pt 4.0pt"><span id="S5.T2.4.4.4.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold" style="font-size:90%;">53.8</span></td>

class="ltx_td ltx_align_center ltx_border_bb ltx_border_t"

class="ltx_td ltx_align_center ltx_border_bb ltx_border_t"

class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold" style="font-size:90%;">47.0</span></td>

class="ltx_td ltx_align_center ltx_border_bb ltx_border_t"

</tbody>
</table>
<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_table"><span id="S5.T2.8.1.1"
class="ltx_text" style="font-size:90%;">Table 2</span>: </span><span
id="S5.T2.9.2" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:90%;"> Standard
benchmark results. Scores denote Avg@3 over three runs with
Gemini-3-Flash as the execution model, and Best R. denotes the first
round reaching the reported best score. The <span id="S5.T2.9.2.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">bold</span> and <span id="S5.T2.9.2.2"
class="ltx_text ltx_framed ltx_framed_underline">underline</span> mark
the best and second-best scores. </span></figcaption>
</figure>

<div id="S5.SS2.p1" class="ltx_para">

<span id="S5.SS2.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Overall
performance.</span> Tables <a href="#S5.T1" class="ltx_ref"
title="Table 1 ‣ 5.2 Main Results ‣ 5 Experiments ‣ Harnessing Agentic Evolution"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">1</span></a>
and <a href="#S5.T2" class="ltx_ref"
title="Table 2 ‣ 5.2 Main Results ‣ 5 Experiments ‣ Harnessing Agentic Evolution"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">2</span></a> show that
<span id="S5.SS2.p1.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">AEvo</span>
consistently improves agentic evolution across both open-ended
optimization and fixed benchmarks. On the open-ended tasks,
<span id="S5.SS2.p1.1.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">AEvo</span>
achieves the best or tied-best result on all three tasks, while also
improving the speed or stability with which strong candidates are found.
In particular, on the Kernel optimization task, <span id="S5.SS2.p1.1.4"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">AEvo</span> achieves 1138 cycles
within 100 iterations, which is, to our knowledge, the best reported
result under the same iteration budget. This suggests that the harnessed
meta-editing loop improves how evolution uses feedback over time, rather
than merely increasing the number of candidate attempts. On standard
agentic and reasoning benchmarks, <span id="S5.SS2.p1.1.5"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">AEvo</span> also improves
procedure-based evolution over strong fixed-loop baselines. The gains
are consistent across both Terminal-Bench and ARC-AGI-2, yielding a 26%
relative improvement over the strongest baseline on average. Together,
these results support the central claim that mechanism-level
intervention can benefit both open-ended optimization and
benchmark-driven agentic evolution.

</div>

<div id="S5.SS2.p2" class="ltx_para">

<span id="S5.SS2.p2.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Improvement
through optimization-time reasoning.</span> The gains on Table
<a href="#S5.T2" class="ltx_ref"
title="Table 2 ‣ 5.2 Main Results ‣ 5 Experiments ‣ Harnessing Agentic Evolution"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">2</span></a> come with a higher per-round
optimization cost. <span id="S5.SS2.p2.1.2"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">AEvo</span> costs about three times
as much as procedure-based baselines on these benchmarks. This means
that <span id="S5.SS2.p2.1.3"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">AEvo</span> improves performance by
scaling the reasoning and deliberation used during optimization, and
supports our view that increasing the budget of the evolution process is
a useful axis for improving agentic evolution.

</div>

<div id="S5.SS2.p3" class="ltx_para">

<span id="S5.SS2.p3.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Cost analysis
and agentic behavior.</span> The open-ended tasks reveal that cost is
not solely determined by whether a method is agent-based or
procedure-based. Agent-based evolution can remain cost-competitive when
implemented through coding-agent interfaces with prompt caching and
persistent contexts. <span id="S5.SS2.p3.1.2"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">AEvo</span> $`{}_{\texttt{Agent}}`$
maintains low per-round cost: 0.34–0.32 on <span id="S5.SS2.p3.1.3"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">circle_packing_26</span>, 1.40–1.31 on
<span id="S5.SS2.p3.1.4"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">autocorrelation_second</span>, and
1.27–1.23 on Kernel optimization. By contrast, procedure-based methods
can become expensive in long-horizon optimization by repeatedly
constructing large prompts over an expanding search history without
comparable caching, as visible in HyperAgents’ higher per-round cost on
Task 1 and Task 2.

</div>

<div id="S5.SS2.p4" class="ltx_para">

At the same time, direct coding agents show why agent freedom alone is
insufficient for reliable evolution. Even with prompts encouraging
long-horizon search, coding agents often stop early once local
improvements become difficult, as seen in the early best rounds of Codex
on Task 1 and Task 3. This suggests that an agent’s internal stopping
decision can conflict with the external evolution budget.
<span id="S5.SS2.p4.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">AEvo</span>
addresses this by placing the coding agent inside an explicit evolution
harness, where rounds, candidate records, and evaluation feedback are
maintained outside the agent’s local decision loop.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S5.SS3" class="section ltx_subsection">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">5.3 </span>Evolution Dynamics

<figure id="S5.F3" class="ltx_figure">
<img src="/html/2605.13821/assets/x3.png" id="S5.F3.g1"
class="ltx_graphics ltx_centering ltx_img_landscape" width="830"
height="381" alt="Refer to caption" />
<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_figure"><span id="S5.F3.5.1.1"
class="ltx_text" style="font-size:90%;">Figure 3</span>: </span><span
id="S5.F3.6.2" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:90%;"> Evolution
trajectories on the <span id="S5.F3.6.2.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">Kernel optimization</span> task. The
left panel compares eight methods over the first 100 iterations, where
blue curves denote <span id="S5.F3.6.2.2"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">AEvo</span> variants. The y-axis
reports the normalized score induced by cycle reduction, so higher is
better; raw iterations and invalid evaluations are shown as scattered
markers. The right panel extends the <span id="S5.F3.6.2.3"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">AEvo</span> run from 100 to 200
iterations and reports raw cycles, where lower is better.
</span></figcaption>
</figure>

<div id="S5.SS3.p1" class="ltx_para">

<span id="S5.SS3.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Revising
evolution after plateaus.</span> As shown in
Figure <a href="#S5.F3" class="ltx_ref"
title="Figure 3 ‣ 5.3 Evolution Dynamics ‣ 5 Experiments ‣ Harnessing Agentic Evolution"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">3</span></a>, procedure-based methods such
as OpenEvolve and HyperAgents tend to flatten once their current
selection or mutation strategy stops producing useful candidates. In
contrast, <span id="S5.SS3.p1.1.2"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">AEvo</span> can revise the mechanism
that drives subsequent evolution. When progress stalls or repeated
failures appear, these signals become process-level feedback: the
meta-agent can adjust the procedure, directive, or reusable search
context, producing step-wise improvements after plateaus. This is
visible in the late-stage jump that leads to the best 100-round result.

</div>

<div id="S5.SS3.p2" class="ltx_para">

<span id="S5.SS3.p2.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Using the
evolution budget effectively.</span> Direct coding agents can obtain
strong early gains through internal simulation, execution, and
debugging, but they may stop early once local progress becomes
difficult. <span id="S5.SS3.p2.1.2"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">AEvo</span> avoids tying progress to
the agent’s local stopping decision by maintaining explicit rounds,
candidate records, and evaluation feedback outside the agent context.
This allows the external evolution budget to be used more consistently.

</div>

<div id="S5.SS3.p3" class="ltx_para">

<span id="S5.SS3.p3.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Scaling beyond
early gains.</span> The right panel extends Codex-based
<span id="S5.SS3.p3.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">AEvo</span>
from 100 to 200 iterations. The best result improves from 1138 to 1121
cycles, showing that <span id="S5.SS3.p3.1.3"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">AEvo</span> continues to benefit
from additional rounds rather than saturating after an early strong
candidate. Overall, the trajectories suggest that agent flexibility is
useful, but reliable long-horizon improvement requires a harness that
preserves global evidence and enables mechanism-level correction.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S5.SS4" class="section ltx_subsection">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">5.4 </span>Ablation Study

<div id="S5.SS4.p1" class="ltx_para">

Table <a href="#A1.T3" class="ltx_ref"
title="Table 3 ‣ Appendix A Ablation Study Details ‣ Harnessing Agentic Evolution"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">3</span></a> (Appendix) ablates two key
components of <span id="S5.SS4.p1.1.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">AEvo</span> $`{}_{\texttt{Agent}}`$
on the Kernel optimization task. The full system completes the 100-round
budget without reward hacking and reaches the best valid result of 1138
cycles. Removing meta-agent skills does not lead to reward hacking, but
it substantially weakens long-horizon search: the best run only reaches
1407 cycles, and the runs do not consistently sustain the full budget.
Removing the evolution harness is even less reliable. Although one run
finds a strong 1167-cycle solution, two of the three runs enter
reward-hacking trajectories and fail to produce valid cycle results.
These results suggest that the skills mainly support sustained and
effective meta-intervention, while the harness provides the protected
evaluation boundary and structured evolution context needed to keep
agentic search aligned with the true objective.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S5.SS5" class="section ltx_subsection">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">5.5 </span>Case Study

<figure id="S5.F4" class="ltx_figure">
<img src="/html/2605.13821/assets/x4.png" id="S5.F4.g1"
class="ltx_graphics ltx_centering ltx_img_landscape" width="830"
height="406" alt="Refer to caption" />
<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_figure"><span id="S5.F4.6.3.1"
class="ltx_text" style="font-size:90%;">Figure 4</span>: </span><span
id="S5.F4.4.2" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:90%;"> Case study of
procedure evolution on an ARC-AGI-2 task. Each <span
class="math inline"><em>P</em></span> denotes a procedure produced or
revised by the meta-agent, and each <span
class="math inline"><em>C</em></span> denotes a candidate agent
generated by the current procedure. Solid nodes are breakthrough
candidates, dashed nodes are failed candidates, and arrows indicate
parent relationships. The meta-agent improves the search process by
changing the procedure across stages, while failed candidates provide
feedback for later interventions. </span></figcaption>
</figure>

<div id="S5.SS5.p1" class="ltx_para">

Figure <a href="#S5.F4" class="ltx_ref"
title="Figure 4 ‣ 5.5 Case Study ‣ 5 Experiments ‣ Harnessing Agentic Evolution"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">4</span></a> illustrates how
<span id="S5.SS5.p1.9.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">AEvo</span>
performs meta-intervention during procedure-based evolution on an
ARC-AGI-2 task. Starting from $`P_{0}`$, the meta-agent initializes a
best-parent rewrite procedure that selects candidate agents by
validation accuracy. This first produces an initial breakthrough
candidate $`C_{1}`$, but subsequent variants expose several failure
modes in observation parsing and refinement. The meta-agent then revises
the procedure rather than continuing the same search blindly: $`P_{1}`$
adds Pass@K sampling and local scoring for verifier-guided generation,
$`P_{2}`$ fixes observation parsing to activate feedback-guided
refinement, and $`P_{3}`$ extends the refinement horizon to use more
pass/fail feedback before submission. When the search becomes stuck,
$`P_{4}`$ drops stale feedback and samples more diverse alternatives,
leading to a stronger candidate. Later interventions $`P_{5}`$–$`P_{6}`$
explore stronger de-anchoring through task-profile and skeleton prompts,
but these regress from $`P_{4}`$. This example shows that failed
candidates are not merely discarded; they become process-level evidence
that helps the meta-agent decide how to revise the future evolution
procedure. Additional evolved procedures, optimized agent harnesses, and
task-level analyses are provided in
Appendix <a href="#A3.SS3.SSS1" class="ltx_ref"
title="C.3.1 Representative Evolved Procedures ‣ C.3 Representative Evolved Artifacts and Outcomes ‣ Appendix C Additional Experimental Details ‣ Harnessing Agentic Evolution"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">C.3.1</span></a> and
Appendix <a href="#A3.SS3.SSS2" class="ltx_ref"
title="C.3.2 Representative Evolved Agent Harness ‣ C.3 Representative Evolved Artifacts and Outcomes ‣ Appendix C Additional Experimental Details ‣ Harnessing Agentic Evolution"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">C.3.2</span></a>.

</div>

</div>

</div>

<div id="S6" class="section ltx_section">

## <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_section">6 </span>Conclusion

<div id="S6.p1" class="ltx_para">

We presented <span id="S6.p1.1.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">AEvo</span>, a harnessed framework
for steering agentic evolution by treating the evolution process itself
as an interactive environment. Instead of generating one more candidate,
<span id="S6.p1.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">AEvo</span>
exposes accumulated candidates, feedback, traces, failures, costs, and
search history as process-level evidence, and uses a meta-agent to edit
the mechanism that controls future evolution. This formulation provides
a unified view of procedure-based and agent-based evolution: the same
meta-editing loop can revise explicit search procedures or the operating
context of general-purpose evolution agents, while keeping evaluation
and candidate recording protected by an external harness. Across
agentic, reasoning, and open-ended optimization tasks,
<span id="S6.p1.1.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">AEvo</span>
improves over strong fixed-procedure and agent-based baselines,
suggesting that long-horizon evolution benefits not only from stronger
candidate generators, but also from mechanism-level intervention over
how search proceeds. Future work should study more diverse evolution
environments, cheaper meta-intervention strategies, and safer deployment
of harnessed agentic evolution for scientific discovery, software
engineering, and autonomous code optimization.

</div>

<div class="ltx_pagination ltx_role_newpage">

</div>

</div>

<div id="A2" class="section ltx_appendix">

## <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_appendix">Appendix B </span>Implementation Details

<div id="A2.p1" class="ltx_para">

When temperature is exposed, we set it to 1; when reasoning-effort
control is available, we use the high setting; and we use a maximum
context budget of 128k tokens. We run 20 optimization rounds on
Terminal-Bench and ARC-AGI-2. On open-ended optimization tasks, we allow
up to 100 rounds and early-stop when a run reaches the known target
score or fails to improve for 25 consecutive rounds. The initial
procedure in the procedure-based setting uses best-valid-candidate
selection plus a heuristic LLM optimizer; details are given in
Appendix <a href="#A3.SS2.SSS1" class="ltx_ref"
title="C.2.1 Initial Procedure in Procedure-Based AEvo ‣ C.2 Initialization Details ‣ Appendix C Additional Experimental Details ‣ Harnessing Agentic Evolution"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">C.2.1</span></a>. The initial agent used on
the standard agentic and reasoning tasks is a
ReAct-style \[<a href="#bib.bib37" class="ltx_ref"
title="React: synergizing reasoning and acting in language models">35</a>\]
agent; details are given in
Appendix <a href="#A3.SS2.SSS2" class="ltx_ref"
title="C.2.2 Initial Agent in Agentic and Reasoning Tasks ‣ C.2 Initialization Details ‣ Appendix C Additional Experimental Details ‣ Harnessing Agentic Evolution"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">C.2.2</span></a>.

</div>

</div>

<div id="A3" class="section ltx_appendix">

## <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_appendix">Appendix C </span>Additional Experimental Details

<div id="A3.SS1" class="section ltx_subsection">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">C.1 </span>Task and Metric Details

<div id="A3.SS1.p1" class="ltx_para">

Our main experiments cover two standard benchmarks and three open-ended
optimization tasks. Terminal-Bench evaluates end-to-end task completion
in terminal environments, while ARC-AGI-2 measures abstract reasoning
under fixed evaluation rules. The three open-ended tasks use hidden or
fixed external evaluators and require the optimizer to improve
executable code rather than only produce one-shot answers.

</div>

<div id="A3.SS1.SSS0.Px1" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### Circle Packing.

<div id="A3.SS1.SSS0.Px1.p1" class="ltx_para">

In <span id="A3.SS1.SSS0.Px1.p1.1.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">circle_packing_26</span>, the goal is
to pack 26 circles into a unit square and maximize the sum of their
radii. The evaluator returns a validity bit and the achieved packing
score. Higher is better.

</div>

</div>

<div id="A3.SS1.SSS0.Px2" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### Autocorrelation-Second.

<div id="A3.SS1.SSS0.Px2.p1" class="ltx_para">

In <span id="A3.SS1.SSS0.Px2.p1.1.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">autocorrelation_second</span>, the goal
is to construct a non-negative function on
$`[-\nicefrac{{1}}{{4}},\nicefrac{{1}}{{4}}]`$ that maximizes

|     |                                                                |     |
|-----|----------------------------------------------------------------|-----|
|     |                                                                
       ``` math                                                        
       R(f)=\frac{\|f*f\|_{2}^{2}}{\|f*f\|_{1}\cdot\|f*f\|_{\infty}}.  
       ```                                                             |     |

The evaluator scores the submitted construction directly by this ratio.
Higher is better.

</div>

</div>

<div id="A3.SS1.SSS0.Px3" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### Performance Engineering.

<div id="A3.SS1.SSS0.Px3.p1" class="ltx_para">

In the performance-engineering take-home, the goal is to optimize a
kernel for a simulated VLIW SIMD machine while preserving correctness on
the hidden tests. We report raw cycle count in the main table, so lower
is better, although the evaluator also exposes a normalized score
derived from the cycle count.

</div>

</div>

<div id="A3.SS1.SSS0.Px4" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### Reporting Protocol.

<div id="A3.SS1.SSS0.Px4.p1" class="ltx_para">

For Terminal-Bench and ARC-AGI-2, we report Avg@3 across three
independent runs, together with the first optimization round that
reaches the best score. For open-ended optimization, we report Best@3
across three runs under a fixed evaluation budget, together with the
first round that reaches the best result and the average dollar cost per
optimization round. Open-ended runs are capped at 100 rounds and are
early-stopped if they reach the known target or plateau for 25
consecutive rounds.

</div>

</div>

<div id="A3.SS1.SSS0.Px5" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### Cost Computation.

<div id="A3.SS1.SSS0.Px5.p1" class="ltx_para">

For a run with $`R`$ optimization rounds, the average dollar cost per
round is

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  | 
``` math
\mathdollar/R=\frac{1}{R}\sum_{r=1}^{R}\left(p_{\mathrm{in}}n^{(r)}_{\mathrm{in}}+p_{\mathrm{cache}}n^{(r)}_{\mathrm{cache}}+p_{\mathrm{out}}n^{(r)}_{\mathrm{out}}\right),
``` |  |

where $`n^{(r)}_{\mathrm{in}}`$, $`n^{(r)}_{\mathrm{cache}}`$, and
$`n^{(r)}_{\mathrm{out}}`$ denote the input, cached-input, and output
token counts in round $`r`$, and $`p_{\mathrm{in}}`$,
$`p_{\mathrm{cache}}`$, and $`p_{\mathrm{out}}`$ are the corresponding
provider prices.

</div>

</div>

</div>

<div id="A3.SS2" class="section ltx_subsection">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">C.2 </span>Initialization Details

<div id="A3.SS2.SSS1" class="section ltx_subsubsection">

#### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsubsection">C.2.1 </span>Initial Procedure in Procedure-Based <span id="A3.SS2.SSS1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">AEvo</span>

<div id="A3.SS2.SSS1.p1" class="ltx_para">

The initial procedure used by procedure-based
<span id="A3.SS2.SSS1.p1.1.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">AEvo</span> is intentionally
minimal. It selects the current best valid candidate as the parent,
applies a single LLM rewrite step, and then invokes the fixed evaluator.
The excerpt below shows the corresponding editable surface.

</div>

<div id="A3.SS2.SSS1.p2" class="ltx_para">

<div id="A3.SS2.SSS1.p2.1"
class="ltx_listing ltx_lst_language_Python ltx_lstlisting ltx_framed ltx_framed_rectangle ltx_listing"
style="--ltx-bg-color:#F7F7F7;">

<div id="A3.SS2.SSS1.p3" class="ltx_para">

This initialization exposes a limited search surface: it uses
score-based selection, a single-parent rewrite step, and no richer
failure analysis. This makes later meta-edits easier to interpret
because they modify these exposed handles rather than the evaluator.

</div>

</div>

<div id="A3.SS2.SSS2" class="section ltx_subsubsection">

#### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsubsection">C.2.2 </span>Initial Agent in Agentic and Reasoning Tasks

<div id="A3.SS2.SSS2.p1" class="ltx_para">

For Terminal-Bench and ARC-AGI-2, the initial agent is a minimal
ReAct-style agent. The prompt surface and the ‘step()‘ loop are both
editable, but the seed version contains only basic reasoning,
short-horizon memory, and a strict JSON+‘bash‘ action protocol.

</div>

<div id="A3.SS2.SSS2.p3" class="ltx_para">

This weakly structured initialization makes subsequent improvements
easier to attribute to changes in prompts, memory management, recovery
logic, or context organization rather than to a highly engineered seed
agent.

</div>

</div>

<div id="A3.SS2.SSS3" class="section ltx_subsubsection">

#### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsubsection">C.2.3 </span>Meta-Agent Skill Excerpt

<div id="A3.SS2.SSS3.p1" class="ltx_para">

The meta-agent is governed by a compact operational skill that
constrains how it reads the workspace, attributes failure, and chooses
one causal intervention at a time. A representative excerpt is shown
below.

</div>

<div id="A3.SS2.SSS3.p2" class="ltx_para">

<div id="A3.SS2.SSS3.p3" class="ltx_para">

The meta-agent therefore acts as a controller over future search rather
than as an additional task-facing worker. The skill keeps that role
boundary explicit.

</div>

</div>

</div>

<div id="A3.SS3" class="section ltx_subsection">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">C.3 </span>Representative Evolved Artifacts and Outcomes

<div id="A3.SS3.SSS1" class="section ltx_subsubsection">

#### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsubsection">C.3.1 </span>Representative Evolved Procedures

<div id="A3.SS3.SSS1.p1" class="ltx_para">

The excerpt below is taken from a high-performing ARC-AGI-2 procedure.
In later rounds, the evolved procedure forwards not only the current
best artifact but also per-task slices from alternative references,
enabling the optimizer to formulate a single causal hypothesis from
those diagnostics.

</div>

<div id="A3.SS3.SSS1.p2" class="ltx_para">

class="ltx_listing ltx_lst_language_Python ltx_lstlisting ltx_framed ltx_framed_rectangle ltx_listing"
style="--ltx-bg-color:#F7F7F7;">

<div id="A3.SS3.SSS1.p4" class="ltx_para">

This excerpt illustrates the main leverage of procedure-mode
<span id="A3.SS3.SSS1.p4.1.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">AEvo</span>: evolution changes the
evidence presented to the optimizer and the way that evidence is
structured, not only the candidate artifact being scored.

</div>

</div>

<div id="A3.SS3.SSS2" class="section ltx_subsubsection">

#### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsubsection">C.3.2 </span>Representative Evolved Agent Harness

<div id="A3.SS3.SSS2.p1" class="ltx_para">

In the agent-based setting, the evolved object is the harness seen by
future inner-agent sessions rather than a single submitted artifact. In
the performance-engineering run, this harness contained at least five
durable layers: a task skill, a session-specific goal, a persistent
family map, support utilities for evaluation accounting, and structured
session notes written back into the workspace.

</div>

<div id="A3.SS3.SSS2.Px1" class="section ltx_paragraph">

class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter" style="font-size:70%;">1140.</span>

</div>

</div>

</div>

<div id="A3.SS3.SSS2.Px5.p2" class="ltx_para">

Together, these excerpts show that the evolved agent harness is not a
single prompt edit. It is a layered control structure consisting of
persistent instructions, hypothesis-carrying goals, accumulated
family-level memory, support code for evaluator interaction, and
structured records that are promoted into future sessions.

</div>

</div>

</div>

<div id="A3.SS3.SSS3" class="section ltx_subsubsection">

#### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsubsection">C.3.3 </span>Representative Optimization Outcomes

<div id="A3.SS3.SSS3.Px1" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### ARC-AGI-2 best artifact.

<div id="A3.SS3.SSS3.Px1.p1" class="ltx_para">

The best ARC-AGI-2 artifact in our run reaches accuracy $`0.35`$
($`7/20`$). Structurally, the final agent has three persistent
components: a prompt surface that distinguishes normal feedback
refinement from “fresh exploration,” a local Pass@K scorer over cached
training pairs, and a validate-versus-submit controller that uses the
best local score and plateau state.

</div>

<div id="A3.SS3.SSS3.Px1.p2" class="ltx_para">

<div id="A3.SS3.SSS3.Px2" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### Analysis.

<div id="A3.SS3.SSS3.Px2.p1" class="ltx_para">

The improvement over the seed agent comes from a tighter coupling
between search control and task-local verification. First, caching the
training pairs removes a brittle dependency on the observation format at
later steps, so local verification remains available throughout the
interaction. Second, Pass@K sampling with prompt diversification
converts a single-sample ReAct loop into a small search procedure over
candidate solvers, with selection driven by observed agreement on the
training pairs rather than by the raw LLM output alone. Third, the
separation between <span id="A3.SS3.SSS3.Px2.p1.1.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">FEEDBACK_SUFFIX</span> and
<span id="A3.SS3.SSS3.Px2.p1.1.2"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">FRESH_EXPLORE_SUFFIX</span> makes
the agent alternate explicitly between exploitation and hypothesis
reset: partial but improving candidates are refined through concrete
failure-conditioned feedback, whereas persistent plateaus trigger a
prompt regime that suppresses anchoring to the current local optimum.
Finally, the validate-versus-submit controller ties action choice to the
best verified score rather than to the latest response, which reduces
premature submission of partially correct programs.

</div>

</div>

<div id="A3.SS3.SSS3.Px3" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### Performance-engineering best artifact.

<div id="A3.SS3.SSS3.Px3.p1" class="ltx_para">

The best performance-engineering artifact is a two-file submission. The
top-level program is only a wrapper; the schedule-level optimization
resides in a benchmark-specialized base that exposes a small set of
round-family control points. The final validated artifact reaches
$`1138`$ cycles by combining evaluator-compatible packaging, explicit
specialization of the benchmark rounds, and a non-uniform assignment of
selector logic across engines.

</div>

</div>

<div id="A3.SS3.SSS3.Px7" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### Analysis.

<div id="A3.SS3.SSS3.Px7.p1" class="ltx_para">

The low cycle count comes from three coupled changes. First, the
file-local <span id="A3.SS3.SSS3.Px7.p1.5.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">importlib</span> wrapper is not
cosmetic: without it, the evaluator rejects the whole family because
sibling modules are not imported through the workspace package path. The
wrapper therefore preserves a modular implementation while keeping the
submission evaluator-compatible. Second, the benchmark-specialized base
restructures the kernel around the exact round pattern of the benchmark
rather than a generic loop. Its control flow is emitted as four
specialized top-of-tree rounds, followed by seven gather rounds,
followed by the same four specialized rounds and a final gather; this
removes generic control overhead where the traversal repeatedly revisits
the upper levels of the tree and makes the period-$`11`$ reuse pattern
directly schedulable. Within each specialized round,
<span id="A3.SS3.SSS3.Px7.p1.5.2"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">emit_hash</span> is emitted
phase-major across tiles, which exposes many independent chains to the
scheduler and improves overlap between <span id="A3.SS3.SSS3.Px7.p1.5.3"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">alu</span>,
<span id="A3.SS3.SSS3.Px7.p1.5.4"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">valu</span>, and memory operations.
Third, the remaining gains come from engine balancing rather than from
further structural refactoring. On this VLIW benchmark,
<span id="A3.SS3.SSS3.Px7.p1.5.5"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">flow</span> has only one slot per
cycle, so replacing a binary selector by
<span id="A3.SS3.SSS3.Px7.p1.5.6"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">multiply_add</span> is profitable
only when the induced <span id="A3.SS3.SSS3.Px7.p1.5.7"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">valu</span> pressure stays below
the new bottleneck. The progression recorded in the run data is
consistent with this view: the explicit-family port yields the dominant
improvement ($`-597`$ cycles), additional
<span id="A3.SS3.SSS3.Px7.p1.5.8"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">madd</span>-based selectors at
depth 1 and depth 3 save another $`29`$ cycles, an
<span id="A3.SS3.SSS3.Px7.p1.5.9"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">alu</span>-based parity/XOR
rebalance saves $`8`$, and the final artifact gains the last $`2`$
cycles by reverting only the second specialized depth-1 selector back to
<span id="A3.SS3.SSS3.Px7.p1.5.10"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">vselect</span>. The best artifact
is therefore not simply a shorter program; it is a schedule in which
specialization, phase ordering, and per-round engine placement are
co-tuned to the simulator’s slot limits.

</div>

<div id="A3.SS3.SSS3.Px7.p2" class="ltx_para">

Taken together, these artifacts illustrate two modes of durable
improvement in <span id="A3.SS3.SSS3.Px7.p2.1.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">AEvo</span>: benchmark tasks improve
through changes in how the optimizer reasons over failures, whereas
open-ended optimization improves through the preservation and
recombination of low-level implementation knowledge across many
sessions.

</div>

</div>
